# Ottawa

This article is about the capital of Canada; not to be confused with [Odawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odawa), [Ottawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawan), [Outaouais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outaouais), [Oshawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oshawa), [Otawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otawa), [Ottoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoa), or [Ottumwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottumwa_\(disambiguation\)). For other uses, see [Ottawa (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_\(disambiguation\)).

**Ottawa** ([/ˈɒtəwə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/6/60/Ottawa.ogg/Ottawa.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/6/60/Ottawa.ogg/Ottawa.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>, [/ˈɒtəwɑː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English); Canadian French: [\[ɔtawɑ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/98/LL-Q150\_%28fra%29-DenisdeShawi-Ottawa.wav/LL-Q150\_%28fra%29-DenisdeShawi-Ottawa.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/98/LL-Q150_%28fra%29-DenisdeShawi-Ottawa.wav/LL-Q150_%28fra%29-DenisdeShawi-Ottawa.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is the [capital city](181337.md) of [Canada](5042916.md). It is located in the [southeastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ontario) portion of the province of [Ontario](22218.md), at the confluence of the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River) and the [Rideau River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_River). Ottawa borders [Gatineau, Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatineau), and forms the core of the Ottawa–Gatineau [census metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census_metropolitan_area) (CMA) and the [National Capital Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Region_\(Canada\)) (NCR).[^cite_note-17] As of 2021, Ottawa had a city population of 1,017,449 and a metropolitan population of 1,488,307, making it the [fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_municipalities_in_Canada_by_population) city and [fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_census_metropolitan_areas_and_agglomerations_in_Canada) metropolitan area in Canada.[^cite_note-\:3-18][^cite_note-19]

Ottawa is the political centre of Canada and the headquarters of the federal government. The city houses numerous [foreign embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Ottawa), key buildings, organizations, and institutions of [Canada's government](616255.md); these include the [Parliament of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada), the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada_Building), the [residence of Canada's viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Hall), and the [Office of the Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Prime_Minister_and_Privy_Council).[^cite_note-20]

Founded in 1826 as "[Bytown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bytown)", [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_corporation) in 1850, and renamed "Ottawa" in 1855,[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] its original boundaries were expanded through numerous annexations and were ultimately replaced by a new city incorporation and amalgamation in 2001. The [municipal government of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Ottawa) is established and governed by the City of Ottawa Act of the [Government of Ontario](2670174.md). It has an elected city council across 24 wards and a [mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Ottawa) elected city-wide, each elected using the [first-past-the-post voting](261709.md) election system.[^cite_note-23]

Ottawa has the highest proportion of university-educated residents among Canadian cities[^cite_note-natpost-24] and is home to several colleges, universities, and research and cultural institutions, including the [University of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Ottawa), [Carleton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carleton_University), [Algonquin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_College), [Collège La Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_La_Cit%C3%A9), the [National Arts Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Arts_Centre), and the [National Gallery of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Canada), as well as [numerous national museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Ottawa), monuments, and historic sites.[^cite_note-25] It has a high standard of living[^cite_note-26] and is one of the most visited cities in Canada, with over 11 million visitors annually.[^cite_note-citynews-27][^cite_note-citizennews-28][^cite_note-29]

## Etymology

The city name "Ottawa" was chosen in 1855 in reference to the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River), whose name is itself derived from the [Algonquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_language) *adawe*, meaning "to trade".[^cite_note-Rayburn2001-30][^cite_note-\:5-31] In modern Algonquin, the city is known as *Odàwàg*.[^cite_note-32]

## History

Main article: [History of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Ottawa)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Ottawa history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Ottawa_history).

### Early history



The [Champlain Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champlain_Sea)

The [Ottawa Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_Valley) became habitable around 10,000 years ago following the natural draining of the [Champlain Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champlain_Sea).[^cite_note-Miller2015-33][^cite_note-34] The first evidence of human presence in the Ottawa Valley are spearpoints dated 8000-8500 [years before present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_Present). By 6000 years before present, there were robust trading and communications networks. Approximately 3000-3500 years before present, there is definitive evidence of continuously existing settlements, including likely hearths and heavy tools. In closer proximity to the modern bounds of the City of Ottawa, there has been documentation of specific settlements at the mouth of the [Gatineau River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatineau_River) dating back to 3000-3500 years prior to post-Columbian contact.[^cite_note-Pilon-35] These findings suggest that these [Algonquin people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_people) were engaged in foraging, hunting and fishing, but also trade and travel. Three major rivers meet within Ottawa, making it an important trade and travel area for thousands of years.[^cite_note-Pilon-35] This period ended with the arrival of settlers and [colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas) of North America by Europeans during and after the 15th century.[^cite_note-36]

### European exploration and early development

In 1610, [Étienne Brûlé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Br%C3%BBl%C3%A9) became the first documented European to navigate the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River), passing what would become Ottawa on his way to the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods19805-37] Three years later, [Samuel de Champlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_de_Champlain) wrote about the waterfalls in the area and about his encounters with the Algonquin people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods19807-38]

The first non-Indigenous settlement in the area was created by [Philemon Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philemon_Wright), a [New Englander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England). Wright founded a lumber town in the area on 7 March 1800 on the north side of the river, across from the present-day city of Ottawa in [Hull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hull,_Quebec).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_de_Wetering1997123-39][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200616-40] He, with five other families and twenty-five labourers, also created an agricultural community called [Wright's Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wright's_Town,_Lower_Canada), which would later become [Gatineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatineau).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor198611-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200620-42] Wright pioneered the [Ottawa Valley timber trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River_timber_trade) (soon to be the area's most significant economic activity) by transporting timber by river from the Ottawa Valley to [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_de_Wetering199711-43][^cite_note-44]

In the 1820s, news of the British military's impending construction of the [Rideau Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Canal) led to land speculation by John Le Breton, a local businessman who bought a land lot on the prediction of the upcoming construction, which led to an alternative canal course being selected.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods198048-52-45] A town was established in 1826 and in 1827 was named after the British military engineer Colonel [John By](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_By), who was responsible for the Rideau Waterway construction project.[^cite_note-\:21-46][^cite_note-\:18-47] The Rideau Canal provided a secure route between [Montreal](7954681.md) and [Kingston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston,_Ontario) on [Lake Ontario](17947.md). It bypassed a vulnerable stretch of the [St. Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_River) bordering the state of New York that had left re-supply ships bound for southwestern Ontario easily exposed to enemy fire during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELegget198622–24-48]



Camp used by soldiers and labourers of the [Rideau Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Canal), on the south side of the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River) in 1826. The building of the canal attracted many land speculators to the area.

Colonel By set up military barracks on the site of today's [Parliament Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill).[^cite_note-49] He also laid out the streets of the town and created two distinct neighbourhoods named "Upper Town" west of the canal and "[Lower Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Town)" east of the canal. Similar to its [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada) and [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada) namesakes, historically, "Upper Town" was predominantly English-speaking and Protestant, whereas "Lower Town" was mostly French, Irish and Catholic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor198631-50][^cite_note-51] Bytown's early pioneer period saw Irish labourers cause unrest during the [Shiners' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiners'_War) from 1835 to 1845, coming into conflict with French settlers.[^cite_note-whc-cpo-52][^cite_note-53]

Bytown's population grew to 1,000 as the Rideau Canal was completed in 1832.[^cite_note-\:21-46] The settlement was incorporated as a town in 1850.[^cite_note-54] In 1855, Bytown was renamed Ottawa and obtained city status.[^cite_note-\:5-31][^cite_note-\:18-47] [William Pittman Lett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Pittman_Lett) was installed as the first city clerk, serving from 1844 to 1891, guiding Ottawa through 36 years of development, leading the hiring of key municipal roles, founding civic organizations, and proposing a set of by-laws for the city.[^cite_note-Cook2015-55][^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57]

Starting in the 1850s, entrepreneurs known as lumber barons began to build large sawmills, which produced tens of millions of board feet of timber, such as producing 39 million in 1855 after the USA began accepting imports, against approximately 480 million board feet imported from across Canada in Britain a decade earlier, and eventually rising to 613 million in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods1980107-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60] [Rail lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bytown_and_Prescott_Railway) built in 1854 connected Ottawa to areas south and, from 1886, to the transcontinental rail network via Hull and [Lachute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lachute), Quebec.[^cite_note-61] By 1885 Ottawa was the only city in Canada whose downtown street-lights were powered entirely by electricity.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63]

### Selection as capital

The selection of Ottawa as a capital city predates the [Confederation of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation). The choice was contentious, with the Parliament of the [United Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Canada) holding more than 200 votes over several decades to attempt to settle on a legislative solution to the location of its capital.[^cite_note-\:6-64] Political dissension between the Reformist and Tory wings of the political establishment led to the [Stony Monday Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stony_Monday_Riot) of 1849. During these riots, the Tories violently disrupted a Reformist reception for the [Governor General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Bruce,_7th_Earl_of_Elgin) to promote Bytown as capital of the province.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin199722-65][^cite_note-66]

The [governor-general of the Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Province_of_Canada) designated [Kingston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston,_Ontario) as the capital in 1841. This was controversial: the cities of [Toronto](64646.md) and [Montreal](7954681.md), as well as the former capital of Lower Canada, [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City), all had legislators dissatisfied with Kingston as the capital, although anglophone merchants in Quebec were the leading group supportive of the Kingston arrangement.[^cite_note-67] In 1842, a vote rejected Kingston as the capital,[^cite_note-68] and study of potential candidates included Bytown, but that option proved less popular than Toronto or Montreal.[^cite_note-69] In 1843, a report by the Executive Council recommended Montreal as the capital, as it was a more fortifiable location and commercial centre. However, the governor-general refused to execute a move without a parliamentary vote.[^cite_note-70] In 1844, the [Queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria)'s acceptance of a parliamentary vote moved the capital to Montreal.[^cite_note-71]

In 1849, after an [Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Order_in_Canada) mob [burned the Parliament building in Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_the_Parliament_Buildings_in_Montreal), several votes were held on a new permanent capital. Kingston and Bytown were again considered. However, the winning proposal was for the legislature to alternate sitting in either Quebec City and Toronto, in a policy known as perambulation.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] Logistical difficulties made this an unpopular arrangement,[^cite_note-74] and an 1856 vote passed for the lower house of parliament to relocate permanently to Quebec City. The move did not proceed, as the [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Council_of_the_Province_of_Canada) refused to approve funding for the relocation.[^cite_note-75]

The funding impasse led to the ending of the legislature's role in determining the seat of government. The legislature requested the Queen determine the seat of government.[^cite_note-\:22-76] The Queen then acted on the advice of her governor general [Edmund Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Walker_Head),[^cite_note-77] who, after reviewing proposals from various cities, selected the recently renamed Ottawa. The Queen sent a letter to colonial authorities selecting Ottawa as the capital, effective 31 December 1857. [George Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Brown_\(Canadian_politician\)), briefly a co-premier of the Province of Canada, attempted to reverse this decision but was unsuccessful. The Parliament ratified the Queen's choice in 1859, with Quebec serving as interim capital from 1859 to 1865.[^cite_note-\:6-64][^cite_note-78] The relocation process began in 1865, with the first session of Parliament held in the new buildings in 1866. The buildings were generally well received by legislators.[^cite_note-79]



Ottawa in 1859, before construction on [Parliament Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill). Two years prior, [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria) selected the city as the permanent capital of the [Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Canada).

Ottawa was recommended by Head as the capital due to palatability with both Upper and Lower Canada.[^cite_note-\:22-76] Other considerations also favoured Ottawa. Ottawa's isolated location, surrounded by dense forest far from the [Canada–US border](630260.md) and situated on a cliff face, would make it more defensible from attack.[^cite_note-McGill-Queen's_Press_-_MQUP-80][^cite_note-Cohen2003-81] Despite the city's regional isolation, there was water transportation access from spring to fall, both to Montreal via the Ottawa River and to Kingston via the [Rideau Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Waterway).[^cite_note-82] Additionally, by 1854 it also had a modern all-season railway (the [Bytown and Prescott Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bytown_and_Prescott_Railway)) that carried passengers, lumber and supplies 82 kilometres (51 miles) to [Prescott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescott,_Ontario) on the [Saint Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_River) and beyond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor198611-41][^cite_note-McGill-Queen's_Press_-_MQUP-80] Ottawa's small size was also thought to make it less prone to politically motivated mob violence, as had happened in the [previous Canadian capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_the_Parliament_Buildings_in_Montreal).[^cite_note-\:12-83] Finally, the government already owned the land that eventually became [Parliament Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill), which it thought would be an ideal location for the Parliament buildings.[^cite_note-\:12-83]

The original Parliament buildings, which included the Centre, East and West Blocks, were constructed between 1859 and 1866 in the [Gothic Revival style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture).[^cite_note-84] [Public Works Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Works_Canada) and its architects were not initially well prepared for the relatively shallow-lying bedrock involved in construction and as a result had to redesign architectural drawings, leading to delays.[^cite_note-\:19-85] The [Library of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Parliament) and Parliament Hill landscaping were completed in 1876.[^cite_note-\:19-85]

### Post-Confederation and early 20th century



[LeBreton Flats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeBreton_Flats) after the [1900 Hull–Ottawa fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Hull%E2%80%93Ottawa_fire). The fire destroyed one-fifth of Ottawa and two-thirds of neighbouring [Hull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hull,_Quebec), [Quebec](7954867.md).

In 1868, the only assassination of a federal Canadian politician happened in downtown Ottawa, on [Sparks Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparks_Street) when [Thomas D'Arcy McGee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_D'Arcy_McGee) was shot, and suspected [Fenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenians) Patrick James Whelan was later convicted and publicly [hanged](155019.md).[^cite_note-86]

In 1874, Métis political leader [Louis Riel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Riel) attempted to take his seat in parliament in Ottawa despite there being an active warrant for his arrest.[^cite_note-87]

In 1889, the government distributed 60 "water leases" to local industrialists, which gave them permission to generate electricity and use hydroelectric generators at [Chaudière Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudi%C3%A8re_Falls).[^cite_note-88] Public transportation began in 1870 with a [horsecar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsecar) system,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_de_Wetering199728-89] overtaken in the 1890s by an [electric streetcar system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_Electric_Railway) that operated until 1959 and peaked at trackage of 90.5km, including an extension to Hull.[^cite_note-90]

In 1900, a chimney fire spread throughout Hull, destroying two-thirds of the city, including the facilities of major lumber employers and main street buildings. Due to high winds, the fire spread quickly through the wooden buildings that were widespread at the time. It eventually spread to Ottawa, where it destroyed about one-fifth of the buildings from the Lebreton Flats south to Booth Street and down to [Dow's Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow's_Lake).[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_de_Wetering199757-92][^cite_note-93] The fire destroyed approximately 3,200 buildings and caused an estimated \$300 million in damage (in 2020 Canadian dollars).[^cite_note-\:20-94] An estimated 14% of Ottawans and 40% of Hull residents were left homeless.[^cite_note-95] The fire had a disproportionate effect on west-end lower-income neighbourhoods. It had also spread among many lumber yards, a major part of Ottawa's economy.[^cite_note-\:20-94]

In 1911, the municipality saw over 80 deaths related to a worse-than-average outbreak of typhoid, caused by sewage disposal affecting the city's drinking water intake from Nepean Bay. Immediate measures taken were insufficient to prevent 1,400 further typhoid cases in 1912, leading to 98 deaths and unsuccessful calls for temporary relocation of the capital to Toronto until the issue was resolved.[^cite_note-96]

A sepia photograph from the fourth floor of a building, overlooking a triangular public plaza, many old cars with canvas tops are parked in the square. Neo-gothic buildings make up two borders of the square, and a set of tram tracks make up the third

Ottawa Post Office, located in Confederation Square, pictured in the early 20th century

On 1 June 1912, the [Grand Trunk Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Railway) opened both the [Château Laurier](489142.md) hotel and its neighbouring downtown [Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Station_\(Ottawa\)).[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_de_Wetering199741-98] On 3 February 1916, the [Centre Block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Block) of the Parliament buildings was [destroyed by a fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill#Fire,_incidents_and_renovations).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHale2011108-99][^cite_note-100] The House of Commons and Senate were temporarily relocated to the recently constructed Victoria Memorial Museum (now the [Canadian Museum of Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Museum_of_Nature))[^cite_note-Mullington2005-101] until the completion of the new Centre Block in 1927. The centrepiece of the new Parliament buildings is a dominant Gothic Revival-styled structure known as the [Peace Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Tower).[^cite_note-(Canada)2004-102][^cite_note-103]

The location of what is now [Confederation Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation_Square) was a former commercial district centrally located in a triangular downtown area surrounded by historically significant heritage buildings, including the Parliament buildings. It was redeveloped as a ceremonial centre in 1938 as part of the [City Beautiful Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_Movement). It became the site of the [National War Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_War_Memorial_\(Canada\)) in 1939 and was designated a [National Historic Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historic_Sites_of_Canada) in 1984.[^cite_note-104] A new [Central Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Post_Office_\(Ottawa\)) (now the [Privy Council of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_Canada)) was constructed in 1939 beside the War Memorial because the original post office building on the proposed Confederation Square grounds had to be demolished.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]

During the [Second World War](32927.md), the City of Ottawa increased temporary housing in the city for men in the military and their families, with the pavilion at [Lansdowne Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lansdowne_Park) being transformed into a barracks, as it had been during the [First World War](4764461.md).[^cite_note-107] The Second World War also coincided with expansion and centralization in federal government operations, with the size of the federal public service roughly tripling from 12,000 to 36,000. This expansion was reflected in the erection of new buildings, both temporary and permanent, to accommodate the new workers.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

Short film by [Canadian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Government) [1938](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938)

### Post–Second World War



V-Day, downtown Ottawa in 1945, to mark the end of [World War II](32927.md)



[Downtown Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Ottawa) is situated on the south bank of the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River) with neighbouring [Gatineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatineau) in the background across the river.



Gréber plan's [National Capital Greenbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Greenbelt) surrounding the urban core



The [John G. Diefenbaker Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_G._Diefenbaker_Building) was Ottawa's fourth city hall, from 1958 [until 2001.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_City_Hall)

Ottawa's former industrial appearance was vastly altered by the 1950 [Gréber Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greber_Plan).[^cite_note-110] Prime Minister [Mackenzie King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mackenzie_King) hired French architect-planner [Jacques Gréber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Greber) to design an urban plan for managing development in the National Capital Region to make it more aesthetically pleasing and a location more befitting for Canada's political centre.[^cite_note-The_Gréber_Report-111] Gréber's plan included the creation of the [National Capital Greenbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Greenbelt),[^cite_note-\:7-112] National Arts Centre,[^cite_note-113] the [Kichi Zibi Mikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kichi_Zibi_Mikan) parkway[^cite_note-114] and [Queensway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensway_\(Ottawa\)) highway system.[^cite_note-\:7-112] His plan also called for the movement of downtown Union Station (now the [Senate of Canada Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada_Building)) to the suburbs,[^cite_note-115] the removal of the street car system, the decentralization of selected government offices, and the relocation of industries and removal of substandard housing from the downtown.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeshenSt-Onge2001360-116]

While not every recommendation in the Grébér Plan was acted upon—for example, city hall was not placed on the east side of the canal—[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]the plan's open space recommendations did lead to the creation of spaces such as the Rideau Canal and Ottawa River pathways.[^cite_note-Erickson2006-119] A major precondition to the creation of the Rideau Canal pathway was the elimination of direct rail service into downtown, leading to the abandonment of the [Ottawa Train Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada_Building) as the city's main station.[^cite_note-The_Gréber_Report-111]

The 1958 National Capital Act established the [National Capital Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Commission) as a [crown corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_corporation). It officially began work in 1959.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121] This marked the creation of a permanent political infrastructure for managing the [capital region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_region). These included plans from the 1899 Ottawa Improvement Commission (OIC), the Todd Plan in 1903, the Holt Report in 1915 and the Federal District Commission (FDC). The National Capital Commission's structure supplanted the Federal District Commission, which was established in 1927 with a 16-year funding commitment but was reliant on the voluntary transfer of municipal planning authorities from the district's municipalities.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]

In 1958, a new [city hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_Hall_\(Ottawa\)) opened on Green Island near Rideau Falls, where urban renewal had recently transformed this industrial location into a green space.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1986186–194-124][^cite_note-125] Prior to this, from 1931 to 1958, city hall had been at the [Transportation Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_Building_\(Ottawa\)) adjacent to Union Station (now part of the [Rideau Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CF_Rideau_Centre)).[^cite_note-126] In 2001, [Ottawa City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_City_Hall) returned downtown to a 1990 building on 110 Laurier Avenue West, the home of the now-defunct [Regional Municipality of Ottawa-Carleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Municipality_of_Ottawa-Carleton). This new location was close to Ottawa's [first (1849–1877)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_City_Hall_\(Ottawa\)) and [second (1877–1931)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_City_Hall_\(Ottawa\)) city halls.[^cite_note-127] This new city hall complex also contained an adjacent 19th-century restored heritage building formerly known as the [Ottawa Normal School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_Normal_School).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1986186–194-124][^cite_note-128]

From the 1960s to the 1980s, there was a large increase in construction in the National Capital Region,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHale2011217-129] which was followed by large growth in the [high-tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-tech) industry during the 1980s and 1990s.[^cite_note-Shavinina2004-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] Ottawa became one of Canada's largest high-tech cities and was nicknamed Silicon Valley North.[^cite_note-133] By the 1980s, Bell Northern Research (later [Nortel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nortel)) employed thousands, and large federally assisted research facilities such as the [National Research Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Research_Council_\(Canada\)) contributed to an eventual technology boom. The early companies led to the establishment of newer firms such as [Newbridge Networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newbridge_Networks), [Mitel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitel) and [Corel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corel).[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

In 1981, Ottawa was the site of the [Kitchen Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitchen_Accord), federal-provincial negotiations that led to [patriation of the Canadian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriation).[^cite_note-136]

In 1991, provincial and federal governments responded to a [land claim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_claim) submitted by the Algonquins of Ontario regarding the unceded status of the land on which Ottawa is situated.[^cite_note-\:13-137][^cite_note-138] Negotiations have been ongoing, with an eventual goal to sign a treaty that would release Canada from claims for misuse of land under Algonquin [title](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_title), affirm rights of the Algonquins, and negotiate conditions of the title transfer, with an agreement in principle arranged in 2016.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-\:13-137]

### 21st Century

Ottawa's city limits have expanded over time, including a large expansion effective 1 January 2001, when the province of [Ontario](22218.md) amalgamated all the constituent municipalities of the [Regional Municipality of Ottawa–Carleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Municipality_of_Ottawa%E2%80%93Carleton) into a single city of Ottawa.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-\:24-142] Regional Chair [Bob Chiarelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Chiarelli) was elected as the new city's first mayor in the [2000 municipal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Ottawa_municipal_election), defeating [Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucester,_Ontario) mayor Claudette Cain.[^cite_note-143] On 15 October 2001, a diesel-powered [light rail transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail_transit) (LRT) line was introduced on an experimental basis.[^cite_note-\:14-144] Known today as [O-Train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O-Train) [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_\(O-Train\)), it was initially dubbed simply "the O-Train", and it connected [downtown Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Ottawa) to the southern suburbs via [Carleton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carleton_University).[^cite_note-\:14-144] The decision to extend the O-Train and to replace it with an electric light rail system was a major issue in the [2006 municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Ottawa_municipal_election), where Chiarelli was defeated by businessman [Larry O'Brien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_O'Brien_\(Canadian_politician\)).[^cite_note-145] After O'Brien's election, transit plans were changed to establish a series of light rail stations from the east side of the city into downtown and for using a tunnel through the downtown core.[^cite_note-146]

In October 2012, the City Council approved the final [Lansdowne Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lansdowne_Park) plan, an agreement with the [Ottawa Sports and Entertainment Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_Sports_and_Entertainment_Group) that saw a new stadium, increased green space and housing and retail added to the site.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] In December 2012, City Council voted unanimously to move forward with the [Confederation Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_\(O-Train\)), a 12.5 km (7.8 mi) light rail transit line, which was opened on 14 September 2019.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151]

In 2020, the city saw a major change in the nature of work due to civil servants without necessary on-site functions moving to online work as a response to the [COVID-19](62750956.md) pandemic, with many government workers shifting to [online work](158555.md).[^cite_note-152] This arrangement continued until the gradual return to in-person work beginning in 2022, when federal workers returned to two mandatory days in-office per week.[^cite_note-153] This would increase to four mandatory days in-office per week in 2026.[^cite_note-154]

In 2022, a movement protesting COVID-19 restrictions called [The Freedom Convoy](69876497.md), drew large numbers of Canadians, including those from outside Ottawa. This led to the [occupation](69876497.md) of [Wellington Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Street_\(Ottawa\)) in Canada's parliamentary precinct, as well as secondary sites outside downtown Ottawa.[^cite_note-\:17-155][^cite_note-156] The movement was associated with the trucking social identity and was preceded by a "Convoy to Ottawa" phase.[^cite_note-157] The occupation began on 29 January and lasted approximately three and a half weeks, ending after the federal government invoked the [Emergencies Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergencies_Act) on 14 February and pursued enforcement action against the protesters, such as [debanking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debanking) of over 250 protestor bank accounts and later carrying out arrests on 19 February.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-\:17-155]

## Geography



Completed in 1913, the [Connaught Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connaught_Building) was constructed in a Gothic Revival style.



Ottawa is located in the southeastern part of Ontario.

Main article: [Geography of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Ottawa)

### Districts and neighbourhoods

Further information: [List of neighbourhoods in Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_neighbourhoods_in_Ottawa)

The present-day city of Ottawa consists of the historic main urban area, as well as other urban, suburban and rural areas within the city's post-amalgamation limits, as well as exurban villages.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

#### Central Ottawa, East End, South End, West End

Central Ottawa and the West, South and East ends form a large portion of the pre-amalgamation city, as well as the former city of [Vanier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanier,_Ontario)—a densely populated, historically Francophone, working class enclave.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] The Prime Minister's official residence, [24 Sussex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24_Sussex_Drive), as well as the *de facto* residence [Rideau Cottage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Cottage) and the Governor General's residence, [Rideau Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Hall) are all located in the New Edinburgh neighbourhood,[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] These areas includ&#x65;**[^](#ref_As_categorized_by_the_list_article_at_the_start_of_the_"Districts_and_Neighbourhoods"_section)** the [downtown core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Ottawa) and other early-developed neighbourhoods to the east, west, and south.[^cite_note-170] These areas include the affluent neighbourhoods of [The Glebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Glebe), Old Ottawa South, [Westboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westboro,_Ottawa), and [Rockcliffe Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockcliffe_Park),[^cite_note-171] and the historically [blue-collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-collar_worker) communities of [Hintonburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hintonburg), [Mechanicsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanicsville,_Ottawa), and [LeBreton Flats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeBreton_Flats).[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] They also include the [ethnic enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_enclave) of [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Ottawa) and [Little Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Italy,_Ottawa).[^cite_note-hoods-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177]

#### Suburbs and outlying communities



Map of Ottawa showing urban areas and names of historical communities

In 1968, the Region of Ottawa-Carleton was formed, creating a [two-tier municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper-tier_municipalities) out of 11 townships, two cities and three villages.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] In the process, the county of Carleton was dissolved and the Township of Cumberland was subsumed from Russell County.[^cite_note-180]

In 2001, the two-tier municipality was converted to a one-tier municipality, merging the regional government and existing lower-tier municipalities into a single city.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-\:24-142]

Ottawa is bounded by the [United Counties of Prescott and Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescott_and_Russell_United_Counties,_Ontario) to the east; by [Renfrew County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renfrew_County,_Ontario) and [Lanark County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanark_County,_Ontario) to the west; by the [United Counties of Leeds and Grenville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeds_and_Grenville_United_Counties) and the [United Counties of Stormont, Dundas and Glengarry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormont,_Dundas_and_Glengarry_United_Counties,_Ontario) to the south; and by the [Regional County Municipality of Les Collines-de-l'Outaouais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Collines-de-l'Outaouais_Regional_County_Municipality,_Quebec) and the City of Gatineau to the north.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]

The main suburban areas extend over ten kilometers from the city's downtown.[^cite_note-gmap-184][^cite_note-185] These areas also include the former cities of Cumberland, Gloucester (with the large suburban district of [Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orleans,_Ontario) outside the [greenbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Greenbelt) split between them), [Kanata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanata,_Ontario), and Nepean.[^cite_note-186] The towns of [Stittsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stittsville,_Ontario) and [Richmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond,_Ontario), part of the former [Goulbourn Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goulbourn_Township,_Ontario), are to the southwest.[^cite_note-hoods-175][^cite_note-187] Nepean, as a suburb, also includes [Barrhaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrhaven,_Ontario).[^cite_note-hoods-175][^cite_note-188] The communities of [Manotick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manotick,_Ontario) and [Riverside South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverside_South,_Ottawa) are on the west side of the Rideau River, and [Greely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greely,_Ontario) is southeast of Riverside south.[^cite_note-hoods-175]

A number of rural communities (villages and [hamlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamlet_\(place\))) are also part of the City of Ottawa.[^cite_note-gmap-184] Some of these communities include [Burritts Rapids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burritts_Rapids,_Ontario); [Ashton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton,_Ontario); [Fallowfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallowfield,_Ontario); [Kars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kars,_Ontario); [Fitzroy Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitzroy_Harbour,_Ontario); [Munster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munster,_Ontario); [Carp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carp,_Ontario); [North Gower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Gower,_Ontario); [Metcalfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metcalfe,_Ontario); [Constance Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constance_Bay,_Ontario) and [Osgoode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osgoode,_Ontario).[^cite_note-hoods-175] Several [towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town) are within the federally defined National Capital Region but outside of Ottawa's municipal boundaries;[^cite_note-gmap-184][^cite_note-\:23-189] including Cantley, Chelsea and Pontiac, and parts of Gatineau, l’Ange-Gardien, [La Pêche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_P%C3%AAche), Notre-Dame-de-la-Salette and Val-des-Monts, and Mississippi Mills.[^cite_note-\:23-189] Even further areas may be part of the Ottawa-Gatineau Census Metropolitan Area, but not part of the City of Ottawa, City of Gatineau, or the National Capital Region, such as North Grenville.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-\:23-189]



Ottawa above the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River) in 2017, left to right— [Byward Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byward_Market) **·**  [Fairmont Château Laurier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmont_Ch%C3%A2teau_Laurier) **·** [National Gallery of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Canada) **·**  [Parliament Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill) with [Library of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Parliament) and [Peace Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Tower) **·** [Alexandra Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandra_Bridge) **·**  [Downtown Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Ottawa) towers **·** [Supreme Court of Canada](123468.md)

### Architecture, built environment and urban planning

Main article: [Architecture of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Ottawa)

Influenced by government structures, much of the city's architecture tends to be formal and [functional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionalism_\(architecture\)). The city is also marked by [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) and [Picturesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picturesque) styles of architecture such as the Parliament Buildings' gothic revival architecture.[^cite_note-RickettsMaitland2004-191] Ottawa's domestic architecture contains single-family homes, but also includes smaller numbers of [semi-detached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-detached) houses, [rowhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowhouse), and [apartment buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartment_building), including those build before the 21st century.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] Many domestic buildings in Centretown are clad in red brick, with trim in wood, stone, or metal. Variations are common, depending on the cultural heritage of the neighbourhoods and the time they were built.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

East of the Rideau Canal and West of the Rideau river lays much of the original footprint of Bytown, specifically [Lower Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Town), a large portion of which is now comprised in the Lowertown West and Byward heritage districts.[^cite_note-\:28-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]

Across the canal to the west lie both [Downtown Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centretown), which grew out of the historical Upper Town portion of Bytown.[^cite_note-\:28-196] [Centretown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centretown) shares a border with Downtown along Gloucester Street.[^cite_note-199] These core neighbourhoods contain streets such as [Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somerset_Street_\(Ottawa\)), [Elgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elgin_Street_\(Ottawa\)) and [Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_Street_\(Ottawa\)), which fill the role of commercial [main streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Street) in the neighbourhood.[^cite_note-200]

Many federal buildings in the National Capital Region are managed by [Public Works Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Works_and_Government_Services_Canada), the federal government's infrastructure management arm, which manages [heritage conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_preservation) in its renovations and management of buildings, such as the renovation of the [Senate Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada_Building).[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203] Downtown Ottawa includes a substantial economic and architectural government presence across multiple [branches of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers). The [legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada)'s work takes place in the parliamentary precinct, which includes buildings on [Parliament Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Hill) and others downtown, such as the [Senate of Canada Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada_Building).[^cite_note-204] Architects involved in the design of the original Parliament Hill buildings include [Thomas Fuller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Fuller_\(architect\)) and [Chillion Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilion_Jones), who designed the now-replaced Centre Block, Thomas Stent and Augustus Laver who designed East and West BLocks, and landscape architecture by Central Park architect Calvert Vaux.[^cite_note-205] Important buildings in the executive branch include the [Office of the Prime Minister and Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Prime_Minister_and_Privy_Council) as well as many civil service buildings.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] The [Supreme Court of Canada](123468.md) building can also be found in this area.[^cite_note-208]

The [skyline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyline) has been controlled by building height restrictions, originally implemented to keep Parliament Hill and the 92.2 meter (302 ft) Peace Tower at [visible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sightline_\(architecture\)) from most parts of the city. The restriction prevented construction of buildings exceeding 150 feet through at least the 1950s, and in the 1960s the limit was raised to just under the Peace Tower's absolute height above sea level.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeshenSt-Onge2001455-209][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] Today, [several buildings are slightly taller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Ottawa-Gatineau) than the Peace Tower structurally, with the tallest being the [Claridge Icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claridge_Icon) at 143 metres (469 ft).[^cite_note-212]

During part of the winter season the Ottawa section of the canal forms the world's largest skating rink, thereby providing both a recreational venue and a 7.8-kilometre (4.8 mi) transportation path to downtown for ice skaters (from [Carleton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carleton_University) to the [Senate of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada_Building) building).[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] On 29 June 2007, the Rideau Canal was recognized as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-215]

Across the Ottawa River, which forms the border between Ontario and [Quebec](7954867.md), lies the city of [Gatineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatineau), itself the result of amalgamation of the former Quebec municipalities of Gatineau, [Hull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hull_\(Quebec\)) and [Aylmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aylmer_\(Quebec\)).[^cite_note-RazinSmith2006-216] Although formally and administratively separate cities in two different provinces, Ottawa and Gatineau (along with several nearby municipalities) collectively constitute the [National Capital Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Region_\(Canada\)), which is considered a single metropolitan area.[^cite_note-\:4-217][^cite_note-218] One federal [Crown corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_corporations_of_Canada), the National Capital Commission, or NCC, has significant land holdings in both cities, including sites of historical and touristic importance.[^cite_note-\:4-217][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] The NCC holds control over 11% of all land in the National Capital Region, including most of the federal lands in the City of Ottawa, but does not control, for example, the Parliament of Canada or the Supreme Court of Canada.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] The National Capital Commission's appropriations powers and its control of large tracts of undeveloped land give the National Capital Commission a great deal of influence over the city's development.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]Around the main urban area is the [National Capital Greenbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Greenbelt), an extensive [greenbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_belt), administered by the NCC for conservation and leisure, and comprising mostly forest, farmland and marshland.[^cite_note-BrownMitchell2005-226]

### Climate

Ottawa has a [warm-summer humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Dfb*)[^cite_note-\:25-227] corresponding to having four distinct seasons.[^cite_note-228] The climate falls in Zone 5a on the Canadian Plant Hardiness Scale.[^cite_note-229]

In January, the coldest month, the average minimum temperature is -14.3°C (6°F).[^cite_note-230] The majority of days in December, January and February typically remain below freezing.[^cite_note-\:26-231] Typically, almost every day of January, February and March, have more than 5 cm of snowpack (29, 28, and 22 days respectively), and on average, approximately 13 days a year see 5 cm or more of snowfall, with 4 of those having over 10 cm.[^cite_note-\:26-231] On average, 232 cm (91 in) of snow falls in a year, mainly from December through March.[^cite_note-\:26-231] Snow occasionally occur in April or October, but snowfall is light and rarely accumulates in significant quantities.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

Outbreaks of polar air often cause frigid temperatures.[^cite_note-233] On average, there are 16 nights in a year in which the temperature falls below -20°C (-4°F). Extremely low temperatures, below -30°C (-22°F), occur on average approximately once every two years.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

Summers are warm and humid.[^cite_note-\:25-227] In July, the hottest month, the average maximum temperature is 27°C (81°F). On average, there are 15 days in a year that have temperatures exceeding 30°C (86°F). Heatwaves are common and may lead to temperatures in excess of 35°C (95°F).[^cite_note-CCN-232] Humidity is generally high at all times of the year, but is most noticeable in the summer. On average, there are 23 days in a year with thunderstorms, with the majority occurring in the summer.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

Spring and autumn, while transitional, can see extremes in temperature. For example, it is not unusual for the month of May to have nights below freezing, nor is it for temperatures to exceed 30°C (86°F).[^cite_note-CCN-232] Transition can move sharply between months; September sometimes sees temperatures in excess of 30°C, while October can see temperatures below freezing, and November temperatures below -10°C.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

On average, days with fog are seen 23 days per year, and they are likely to occur in all seasons.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

Annual rainfall averages around 757 mm (29.8 in), while total precipitation averages 930 mm (36.6 in). Days with any precipitation is spread fairly evenly throughout the year, but high precipitation days are more common in late summer and early fall.[^cite_note-CCN-232]

In a typical year, Ottawa experiences about 2,080 hours of sunshine annually (45% of possible), with June, July and August being the sunniest period after accounting for time between sunrise and sunset.[^cite_note-Ottawa_CDA1981-234] Prevailing winds are generally from the [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerlies).[^cite_note-\:8-235]

### Physical geography

Ottawa is situated on the south bank of the Ottawa River and contains the mouth of the [Rideau River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_River) and a terminus of the [Rideau Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rideau_Canal).[^cite_note-RipleyDana1875-242][^cite_note-243] The Rideau Canal (Rideau Waterway) first opened in 1832 and is 202 kilometres (126 mi) long.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] It connects the Saint Lawrence River on Lake Ontario at Kingston to the Ottawa River near Parliament Hill.[^cite_note-246] The canal was able to bypass the unnavigable sections of the [Cataraqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cataraqui_River) and Rideau rivers and various small lakes along the waterway due to flooding techniques and the construction of 47 water transport [locks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lock_\(water_transport\)) between the Ottawa River and Lake Ontario.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

Ottawa is situated in a lowland on top of [Paleozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleozoic) carbonate and shale and is surrounded by more craggy [Precambrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precambrian) igneous and metamorphic formations.[^cite_note-\:27-251] Ottawa has had fluvial [deposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deposition_\(geology\)) of [till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Till) and sands, leading to the widespread formation of [eskers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esker).[^cite_note-\:27-251] There are limited distinct features arising from glacial deposits, but Ottawa was affected by the [Late Wisconsian advance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_glaciation).[^cite_note-\:27-251] Before the draining of the Champlain Sea, the area had high salinity.[^cite_note-\:27-251] After the draining of the sea, the area had pine-dominated forests.[^cite_note-\:27-251]

Ottawa is located within the [Western Quebec Seismic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Quebec_Seismic_Zone), and while relatively inactive, the city does occasionally experience earthquakes.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-Earthquake2000-253][^cite_note-254][^cite_note-Earthquake2010-255][^cite_note-256]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Ottawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Ottawa)
